## Version 2.113.0 (2026-08-03)

### New Features
- **Terrestrial refraction: the solid scene is lofted, not just the sky** (`f428511b`, merged as `090cf797`; `src/atmosphere/terrestrialRefraction.js`, `src/atmosphere/refractionSettings.js`, `src/js/map33/material/DayNightStandardMaterial.js`, `src/js/map33/material/TerrainDayNightMaterial.js`, `src/nodes/CNodeView3D.js`, `src/LongExposure.js`). Only celestial objects were refracted, so terrain, 3D tiles, buildings and the sea drew at their geometric positions against a sky that had been lifted. Reported as a Moon photographed over Malmö from Copenhagen: the Turning Torso rendered below where the photograph put it. Light from a target a finite distance away has only crossed the atmosphere between observer and target, so the bend is set by RANGE, not by Saemundsson's whole-atmosphere integral: a ray through the stratified lower atmosphere has near-constant curvature `k/R`, and the angle between the chord to the target and the tangent at the eye is half the total bend, `dTheta = k*d/(2R)` — 0.7' at 20 km, 3.4' at 100 km — the same first-order geometry as the surveyors' effective earth radius `R/(1-k)`. Beyond that range constant-`k` fails, so the angle saturates smoothly at `maxBendRad` = 34'. Applied in VIEW space as a clip-position-only warp: view space because scene coordinates are ECEF (~6.4e6 m) where float32 resolves about half a metre and a 4 m lift added in world space quantises to nothing; clip-only because refraction bends light rather than raising the land, so world positions, normals, shadow coordinates, `raycastGroundElevationFast`, `groundBelow`, AGL and LOS traverse all stay geometric. With `k = 0` the shader reduces to `projectionMatrix * mvPosition` exactly, so a patched-but-disabled material renders bit-identically — which is what lets `installTerrestrialRefractionOnMaterial` be installed unconditionally at material creation with no walk-the-scene-and-recompile on toggle, and why every terrain, ocean and Google-tiles pixel baseline stays at 0px. Covered: Google Photorealistic and Cesium OSM tiles (via `DayNightStandardMaterial`, which `TilesDayNightPlugin` swaps in for every streamed tile, so late-arriving tiles are handled too), the terrain mesh, the ocean surface, and two override-material passes that would otherwise leave their silhouettes geometric — the aerial-perspective depth prepass and the long-exposure occlusion mask. Not covered yet: glTF models, tracks and LOS lines. `k` is derived rather than free: refractivity goes as P/T, and differentiating through the hydrostatic relation gives `k = 503 * (P/T^2) * (0.0342 + dT/dh)`, so two of its three inputs are the same air the Saemundsson path already uses and only the surface temperature gradient is new — which is also where the two models genuinely part company, since the astronomical integral is dominated by the free troposphere while a terrestrial ray never leaves the surface layer, where dT/dh runs from -34 K/km (autoconvective) to +50 K/km (inversion) and swings `k` from 0 to over 0.5. Note the traditional surveying `k = 0.13` is not standard air — it implies dT/dh = -13.7 K/km, a sun-warmed land surface; a standard lapse gives 0.176, and an inversion over water (routine at night, and the Øresund case exactly) gives 0.3 or more, 1.8' at 20 km rather than 0.7'. All refraction UI now lives in **View → Atmospheric Refraction**: *Enable Refraction* (master), *Terrain and Buildings* and *Sky* (both on), *Refraction Pressure (hPa)*, *Refraction Temperature (°C)*, *Surface Temp Gradient (K/km)* (default -6.5), *Refraction Coefficient k* (read-only unless overridden) and *Override k*. `Sit.refractionEnabled` and `Sit.terrestrialRefraction` are now derived from the master and their checkbox in `applyRefractionMaster()`, so no consumer changed; `ensureRefractionSettings()` migrates a sitch saved with the old single *Atmospheric Refraction* checkbox to master-on with both halves on, which means one that wanted a refracted sky now gets refracted ground under it. The folder is built by `setupRefractionGUI()` in `refractionSettings.js` rather than by either owning node, because either can be absent (a star-only sitch has no `CNodeTerrainUI`, a terrain-only one has no `CNodeDisplayNightSky`) — both call it and whichever runs first builds it. `updateTerrestrialRefractionUniforms` inverts `camera.matrixWorld` itself rather than reading `camera.matrixWorldInverse`, because Three only refreshes that cached inverse inside `renderer.render()` and every caller here runs before it, which would leave the bend axis a frame stale on a moving camera and flatly wrong in the long-exposure mask (which re-points the camera before rendering); a test in `tests/terrestrialRefraction.test.js` gives the fake camera a deliberately wrong cached inverse so reinstating that read fails loudly. Verified in-app by cross-correlating the Turning Torso's shaft between renders at 20.573 km: 0.715' measured against 0.720' predicted at k = 0.13, and 2.758' against 2.760' at k = 0.50, with the Moon moving 0.00 px in both.
- **Mask Ground (auto) — seedless sky/ground classification by quadtree** (`0ece968a`; `src/SkyMask.js` `quadTreeGround` / `quadTreeGroundMask`, `src/SkyMaskTool.js` `applyQuadTreeGroundMask`, button *Mask Ground (auto)* in **Video → Masking**). A one-click ground mask with no seeds, and the first approach that works on a wide night landscape with light glow along the horizon — the image where seeded region growing declined on both cues. Every failure the growing route produced was a CONNECTIVITY failure rather than a classification one: a fill seeded in a vignette's dead surround inverted sky and ground; a fill that could not cross a brightness ramp abandoned a good sky's darker corner; a gate tight enough to seal foliage sealed everything; and a lace of leaf edges let the fill leak into tree interiors. Judging each block on its own pixels removes all four, because nothing has to reach anything. The heuristic is that sky is whatever a LARGE uniform block can describe: blocks subdivide while they are busy, so foliage splits to the limit while open sky stops early, and a block's size becomes a measure of how busy its neighbourhood was — which is what separates clear sky from the smooth dark interior of a tree, a distinction no fill can make since that interior is genuinely smooth and genuinely enclosed. Uniformity is measured on the TEXTURE field, not brightness: the spread of absolute brightness across a block is dominated by the scene's smooth gradients (glow rising to a horizon, vignetting), so blocks failed in bands wherever the glow changed fastest and produced a checkerboard across clean sky; texture is a local 3x3 spread, so a ramp barely registers and only structure does. The block statistic is its 90th-percentile texture rather than the mean, because a treetop occupying a fifth of a block must condemn it and a mean dilutes that away. Neighbours then vote — a soft prior, not a return to connectivity: a genuinely different block survives a clear majority and nothing can be severed from a seed because there is no seed. The voting is asymmetric, and so is the final boundary growth: half the neighbours suffice to pull a block down to ground, promoting one back to sky takes 85%, and the finished ground grows by one block into its mixed boundary — missing ground puts hundreds of foliage detections into the solve, while over-masking sky costs a few stars out of thousands.
- **Mask Ground (click sky, then ground) — seeded region growing with a measured cue** (`da9febdd`, hardened by `78005dca`; `src/SkyMask.js` `growSkyFromSeeds` / `chooseFeature` / `skyFeatures`, `src/SkyMaskTool.js` `startMaskGroundPick` / `applyGroundMask`, button *Mask Ground (click sky, then ground)* in **Video → Masking**). On the twilight treeline photo this takes the mask from hand-painted to one gesture: 38.6% of the frame masked, treeline, intruding leaf and overhead wire all covered, star field untouched. SEEDED, NOT AUTOMATIC, because the standard "sky is whatever touches the top border" assumption does not merely bend on Sitrec footage, it inverts: an image intensifier images a circular active area onto black, and the dead surround is the SMOOTHEST region in the frame (texture 0.00 against the sky's 0.63) while filling 76% of the top row, so a top-seeded fill floods the surround, the circle's bright edge seals the real sky off from the seed, and the star field comes back classified as ground. TWO clicks because the cue is measured, not assumed: texture separates sunlit foliage sixteenfold and is useless on a dark sea horizon where brightness carries it, so with a sample of each class `chooseFeature` simply measures the separation and uses the better feature — on the tree photo it picks luma, scoring 64.95 against texture 0.26. Growth is gradient-limited rather than windowed about the seed (a neighbour joins if it is close to the pixel it joins FROM), which follows a smooth ramp — vignetting, airglow — for as far as the ramp goes while stopping at a step; gating on distance from the seed instead masked the darker corner of a perfectly good sky. Two operations the daylight literature has no reason to include: a median at the working scale removes point sources, because a star is an outlier there and survives downsampling as a bump that clears any threshold low enough to catch foliage (without it, every star was masked); and a morphological closing seals the textured region before the fill, because foliage edges form a lace rather than a wall and the fill leaks into dark tree interiors (without it, the treeline masked 0.52 instead of 0.96). `78005dca` added two guards from a wide night landscape with horizon glow: the cue is chosen from the seed windows, which can only measure separation LOCALLY, so a split that fails the region check now retries with the other cue before giving up (at working width, so it costs little at any source resolution, with a `forced` flag terminating it after exactly one attempt); and that retry exposed a hole in the check itself, which required both regions to be non-empty and so let a growth that reached almost nothing skip the test entirely and return a confident 100%-masked frame — growing no sky from a seed the user placed on sky now reads as the failure it is. KNOWN LIMITATION, stated in the tooltip: footage through a vignetted optic can over-mask — on the PVS14 clip the sky's own brightness falls 58.7 to 30.4 from centre to edge while the ground below the horizon sits at 23.7, so the classes overlap and no single cue separates them. The operation is user-invoked, previewable and undoable, and ADDS to the mask rather than replacing it, so a bad result costs one *Clear Mask*.
- **Star Tracker skips detections inside the video mask** (`e9f63ce1`; `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`, *Use mask* in **Video → Star Tracker**, default on). The treeline photo detected ~1000 "stars", roughly 40% of them lit foliage, and identification could not hold consensus against that; with the ground masked it names 221. Measured: detections kept as stars 1047 → 423, stars in the tree region ~409 → 0, identification failed → 221 matched. Reads the shared `"videoMask"` node, resolved ONCE per run — the mask is a user artefact that should not change mid-pass, and refreshing its pixels per frame would cost a `getImageData` on every one. Coordinates are scaled into the mask canvas (`maskCanvas.width / px.W`) rather than indexed directly, because detections live in the ANALYSED decode space, which is not the space the mask was painted in whenever *Max Resolution* caps the decode — a 4K clip analysed at 1080p would otherwise reject a quadrant of the frame and keep the foliage. Masked detections are tallied as `rejectCounts.masked` alongside blended, elongated and the rest so "why is that star not circled" stays answerable, and the live preview draws what SURVIVED rather than what was detected, since otherwise a working mask looks broken. Not done: the detector still looks inside masked regions, so those pixels still contribute to the local background estimate near the mask boundary — pushing the mask down into `detectSources` would fix that for a much larger change than the remaining error justifies.
- **Star Tracker shows its working during a run** (`47dfc1a4`; `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`, `src/starTrack/StarCalibrate.js`, `src/starTrack/StarIdentify.js`, `src/starTrack/StarSphereSolvePool.js`, `src/par.js`; *Display during analysis* in **Video → Star Tracker**, default on). Detection circles each frame's accepted blobs — raw detections, drawn all alike, since nothing is solved yet. The lens fit draws a crosshair at the optical axis it currently believes in, tethered to the frame centre so the offset is the visible quantity. The spherical solves report the residual against the step size and the tolerance that stops the loop, which answers "nearly done or stuck" (a residual sparkline was tried first and cut, as 4-12 unlabelled points carry about one bit). Identification draws its verified quads, five best kept in distinct light colours with line weight scaling with how much of the field each explains, and they persist after the search because on a good map it finishes in under 50 ms and anything shown only during it is invisible. `solveField` becomes async with optional `onYield`/`onCandidate` hooks so the search can breathe, and `calibrateLens` and the spherical pool gain progress reports; omit the hooks and every solver runs exactly as before. Playback is paused and LOCKED for the duration, because the detect pass drives `par.frame` itself and live playback advancing the same counter would interleave with it; a completed run parks on the first analysed frame and stays paused. Stars the catalog gave a proper name to always draw at full strength however faint, since a name at quarter opacity is unreadable. *Measure/Analyze/Identify/Sync* is renamed *Full Analysis*, and *Analyze In/Out range* is renamed *Find Candidate Stars*.
- **[ADMIN, EXPERIMENTAL] Mask Ground with AI** (`src/SkyMaskAI.js`, `sitrecServer/aimask.php`, `src/CMotionAnalysisUI.js`; *[ADMIN] Mask Ground with AI* and *[ADMIN] AI Margin* in **Video → Masking**). Sends the current frame — scaled to at most 1024 px wide (never upscaled), JPEG at quality 0.85, bare base64 — to the user's selected model (`Globals.settings.chatModel`, set in **Settings → AI Model**, split into `provider:model`) and asks it to OUTLINE the sky as polygons in normalised coordinates; everything on the other side of that outline is added to the mask. This asks the model for the ANSWER, not a hint towards one: an earlier iteration asked for SEED POINTS to drive `growSkyFromSeeds` and was built and then abandoned, because on a wide night landscape the sky's own variation (dark zenith to bright light-pollution glow) is as large as the difference between sky and ground, so a region grown on a single cue cannot separate the classes — measured on the treeline test frame, three sources of visually correct seeds all fell below the segmenter's separation gate of 1.0 (gpt-5-mini 0.69, a hand-placed human pair 0.63, claude-opus-4-8 0.08), and forcing the gate open produced a nearly INVERTED mask. The seeds were never the problem. `readPolygon` treats the response as untrusted text: a polygon survives only with ≥3 finite points all within `COORD_TOLERANCE` (0.05) of the unit square, and a model that answered in PIXELS is rejected outright rather than clamped, since clamping would collapse the outline into one corner. The outline is rasterised at `SKY_MASK_DEFAULTS.workWidth`; a `"sky"` region means "mask everything OUTSIDE this", stated in one path via the even-odd fill rule (whole frame as the outer ring, each sky polygon as a hole), while a `"ground"` region fills nonzero. *[ADMIN] AI Margin* (0–0.05, step 0.002, default 0.01) is a safety band stroked ALONG that same path with `lineWidth = 2 * margin * Math.min(W, H)`, which widens the masked side by half the line width whichever side that is, so one stroke serves both region kinds without a separate inset/outset pass. The result composes through `mask.applyMaskEdit("Mask Ground (AI)", …)`, so it is undoable and ADDS to hand-painted work and to any OSD or redaction masking. The video is captured before the request and identity-checked after it, so an outline traced on footage that has since been replaced is discarded rather than painted onto the new clip. Failures are surfaced via `showError` rather than logged — no model selected, a text-only model, a provider outage — and include what the AI said, since on this path the first question is always whether it read the scene right. The button renames itself to *Asking the AI…* while in flight and refuses re-entry, and a success switches on *Enable Mask* and *Edit Mask*, because a rough outline is EXPECTED to need tidying. The client-side `isAdmin()` gate only hides the button; the check that matters is `sitrecServer/aimask.php`, which requires admin, caps the base64 payload at 1.6 MB, re-validates `provider`/`model` against the same permission table chatbot.php uses via `isModelAllowed`, and rejects any provider outside `['anthropic', 'openai', 'gemini']` with a message naming the models that would work rather than passing an image to a text-only model.
- **Second stage: the image places the boundary the model only approximated** (`refineGroundByLocalDarkness` in `src/SkyMask.js`). A vision model can say which side of a frame is ground — the semantic question — but places the boundary itself several percent of the frame out, routinely leaving a tree's canopy on the sky side of its polygon. After the AI outline is rasterised, the ground region is grown into whatever is locally darker than a local sky background. The word local is doing all the work: a global brightness threshold is exactly what defeated `growSkyFromSeeds` on these frames, since the sky runs from a near-black zenith to a bright horizon glow, a range wider than the gap between sky and trees. The background is a box-filtered mean (`boxSums`, separable running totals, radius `refineRadiusFrac` = 8% of working width) taken over CLAIMED-SKY PIXELS ONLY — sums of sky luma divided by sums of the sky mask — so the trees cannot drag it down and it follows the horizon-glow gradient; windows holding fewer than `refineMinWindowSky` (20) sky pixels fall back to the global sky mean. A pixel joins the ground if it falls below its local background by `max(refineK * mad, refineMinDrop)` = `max(2.5 × sky MAD, 4 luma)`, where the MAD is the sky's own mean absolute deviation from that same local background, keeping the threshold scale-free so a grainy high-ISO frame earns a wider gate instead of having its noise called foliage. Growth is a 4-connected BFS out of the EXISTING ground rather than a global sweep of dark pixels, because the features being recovered (a canopy attached to its trunk, a ridge attached to the bottom of the frame) are all joined to ground the model already identified, and requiring that join is what stops a genuinely dark patch of sky being masked on its own. One dilation (`refineDilate` = 1 working pixel) finishes, because a silhouette's edge pixels are a blend of tree and sky, sit above the threshold, and would otherwise survive as a one-pixel fringe of "sky" tracing every branch — the worst possible sky to keep, being exactly where a false detection would land. Bails with `"almost no sky claimed"` when the claimed sky is under `minSkyFraction` (2%).

### Improvements
- **The video mask is now shared, not owned by Motion Analysis** (`05b130bd`; `src/nodes/CNodeMaskOverlay.js`, `src/CustomManagerSetup.js`, `src/CMotionAnalysis.js`, `src/CMotionAnalysisUI.js`, `src/SitchMigrations.js`, `src/RegisterSitches.js`). The mask began as a Motion Analysis feature and stayed one, so anything else wanting to ignore part of the frame had to instantiate a whole `MotionAnalyzer` to hold a mask — which is what `ensureMaskingAnalyzer()` exists to do. The Star Tracker needs the same pixels ignored, so the node now builds as `"videoMask"` in `CustomManagerSetup`, unconditionally for every custom sitch with a video, on the same terms as the annotate overlay beside it. Not lazily, and this is the load-bearing part: a node that does not exist when the saved mods are applied never receives its state at all, so a mask created on first use would save correctly and then silently fail to reload. `CNodeMaskOverlay`'s single `onMaskChange` slot becomes a listener set — one callback quietly made whoever CONSTRUCTED the node its owner, which is how the coupling arose — and Motion Analysis now subscribes like anyone else and adopts the shared node, with a private node still built for sitches that have no shared one. The **Masking** folder moves out of the Motion Analysis submenu to sit beside it directly under **Video** (`createMaskingFolder(guiMenus.video)`), since the mask is no longer that feature's property. Four invariants are now written down on the node, because each was assumed differently somewhere: polarity (alpha > 128 means EXCLUDED — the opposite of the "keep" masks OpenCV wants, and of `CameraMotionFromVideo`'s own `buildMask`), coordinate space (video pixels, not display, and not necessarily the size an analysis ran at), video identity (one view can switch sources while keeping one canvas), and composition (automatic masking ADDS to hand-painted work rather than replacing it). Old saves carry their painted mask across via `migrateMaskOverlayId` in `SitchMigrations.js`, which renames the mod key — without it the mod would be applied to a node that no longer exists and the mask would quietly vanish on reload.
- **Mask brush maximum scales with the video's resolution** (`src/nodes/CNodeMaskOverlay.js` `maxBrushSize()` / `BRUSH_MAX_AT_720` = 100, `src/CMotionAnalysisUI.js` `syncBrushSliderMax`). `brushSize` is measured in the source's own pixels, so a fixed ceiling meant something different on every clip: 100 px is a broad stroke on 720p and a dot on a 4032-wide stills camera, where painting out a treeline by hand would take hundreds of passes. The ceiling is now `max(100, round(100 × videoHeight / 720))`, keeping the brush the same FRACTION of the picture whatever the resolution — which is what the hand expects, since the user sees the video scaled to fit a view either way. Both consumers follow it: the `]` key clamps to `maxBrushSize()` instead of a literal 100, and the *Brush Size* slider's max is re-read on entering *Edit Mask* (both when the brush starts to matter and after any video swap that changed the resolution) instead of sitting at a fixed 50. The two ceilings have to track each other or they disagree damagingly: `]` could take the brush past a fixed slider max, and then merely touching the slider would clamp the value back down. The step already grows as `1 + sqrt(brushSize)/2`, so the ends of the enlarged range stay reachable in a similar number of presses.
- **AI masking requests appear in the admin dashboard alongside chat requests** (`sitrecServer/ai_models.php` and `sitrecServer/ai_log.php`, both new; `sitrecServer/chatbot.php`, `sitrecServer/admin_dashboard.php`, `sitrecServer/aimask.php`). The LLM model-permission table (`$MODEL_PERMISSIONS`) plus `getApiKeyForProvider` and `isModelAllowed` are extracted from `chatbot.php` into `ai_models.php`, and `$AI_LOG_FILE` plus `logAIRequest` into `ai_log.php`. Shared for the same reason in both cases: the log is only useful if EVERY endpoint that spends money on a provider writes to it, and a second endpoint keeping its own file — or a reader naming the same path separately — produces an admin dashboard that quietly under-reports. `admin_dashboard.php` now requires `ai_log.php` for the path rather than redeclaring it, and `aimask.php` calls `logAIRequest($userId, '[mask ground] sky outline from video frame', $model)` under the same `SITREC_TRACK_STATS` switch chatbot.php uses. No change to chat behaviour or to which models any tier can reach.

### Bug Fixes
- **Fixed a view saved full-window but not fullscreen, stuck covering the layout** (`899b8540`; `src/nodes/CNodeView.js` `modSerialize`, `src/CViewManager.js` `restoreFullscreenFromMods`). Loading a saved sitch could show the video as a single quadrant of the frame. Not a scaling bug: the video VIEW was 1x1 — the whole window — drawn beneath the main and look views, so the only visible part was the corner nothing else covered. `modSerialize` suppresses `doubled` on any view that is not the current fullscreen owner, so several views cannot each claim fullscreen on load, but it cleared the FLAG while leaving the fullscreen GEOMETRY in left/top/width/height. That writes a state which cannot exist and cannot be recovered from — not fullscreen, yet filling the window — and nothing un-doubles it on load either, because `undouble()` returns early on `doubled` (the flag just cleared) and no fullscreen owner exists to release it, so the view stays 1x1 for the life of the sitch. The affected sitch had exactly that: live geometry 0,0,1,1 with `doubled` false and `preDoubled` 0.5,0.002,0.5,0.499 sitting right there unused. Serialization now rewinds the geometry along with the flag, saving the view where it would be un-doubled, and `restoreFullscreenFromMods` repairs saves already written the broken way, which previously fell through its "no fullscreen, nothing to do" branch. The repair is deliberately narrow, because a view legitimately can fill the window when it is the only thing on screen: it fires only when another independent, non-full, non-overlay view is visible — so a full-window tile contradicts the layout around it — and it warns to the console when it acts.
- **Fixed the first pasted image colliding with its own reservation** (`b4023f01`; `src/DragDropHandler.js`). Pasting an image into a clean sitch asked "pasted-image-1.png has already been imported" on the very first paste, with nothing imported at all. Two features added in 2.112.0 were both naming files: `getPastedFiles` minted `pasted-image-1.png` and reserved it via `reserveUniqueName`, and `resolveNameCollision` then found that name reserved — by itself, moments earlier — and reported a clash. Each feature was tested and worked; the edge between them was never exercised, because the paste tests predate the collision prompt and the collision tests called `uploadDroppedFile` directly rather than going through `onPaste`. Naming now has one owner: `renameGenericPastedFile` is deleted, `getPastedFiles` returns the clipboard's files untouched, and `resolveNameCollision` renames a clipboard placeholder (regex `^image\.[a-z0-9]+$`) silently — that name carries no information, so there is nothing for the user to decide — and prompts only for names a user actually chose. Verified through `onPaste`: a first paste into an empty sitch imports silently; two images in one paste become -2 and -3 alongside an existing -1; and dropping a file genuinely named `pasted-image-1.png` still prompts.
- **Fixed Star Tracker anchoring its quads in foliage** (`47dfc1a4`; `src/starTrack/StarIdentify.js`). On a twilight photo with a treeline every quad was anchored in foliage — measured, 24 of 25 anchors in the trees. The existing density guard did nothing: its bar is 4x the median neighbour count, the sky was itself densely detected (median 14), and the foliage at ~50 never reached 56, so it excluded nothing at all. The cause was the ranking. Anchors were the brightest detections by integrated flux, and lit foliage is bright because a clump is LARGE — on that image the tree blobs and the sky stars have comparable areas but peakSNR medians of 11.5 against 33.5, so the stars are far stronger per pixel while the leaves win on total light. Anchors are now drawn only from compact round detections and ranked by peak SNR: compactness alone leaves 19 of 25 in the trees, SNR alone 12, both together 1. The extent bar is relative to the image's own median detection so it carries across sensors, and the elongation bar is tighter than the detector's own admission limit. All three measurements are optional — callers passing bare `{x, y, mag}` keep the previous behaviour exactly, which is what the headless tests exercise. The good clip improved too: the rotating-starfield clip goes 72 → 89 matches. The tree photo now declines honestly ("lost the match consensus") rather than confidently naming foliage; identifying that very wide field is a separate problem.
- **Fixed a mask from the previous video being stretched onto newly imported media** (`src/nodes/CNodeVideoView.js` `makeImageVideo`, `src/nodes/CNodeVideoWebCodecView.js` `uploadFile`). A mask belongs to the picture it was drawn on, but `initMask()` carries an existing mask forward by SCALING it into the new canvas, so one left behind did not merely linger — it was stretched onto a picture whose horizon is somewhere else entirely. Both import paths now clear the `"videoMask"` node before the new media is installed. The clear sits in `makeImageVideo` rather than at the import call sites because there are several of those (drag-drop, the file menu, the parse pipeline) and they all arrive there. Guarded so a restore keeps what was saved with it: the image path requires `!Globals.deserializing && restoreIndex === undefined`, and the video path `!Globals.deserializing` (`uploadFile` is only reached from a user import).
- **Fixed an edited mask brush size never being restored** (`05b130bd`; `src/nodes/CNodeMaskOverlay.js`). `brushSize` now serializes with the mask. It was written to the mask node and read back off the analyser — exactly the drift that comes of one system owning what another configures.

### Documentation
- **`docs/StarTracker.md` — the Star Tracker user manual** (`c1886adb`). Quickstart, glossary, how to read the overlay, a GUI reference, and an account of the algorithms: detection, the frame chain, tracklets and classification, the lens fit and why it matters, cluster grouping, and quad-code identification. Five screenshots from real analyses and six SVG diagrams in `docs/star-images/`. Reviewed twice before landing, which was worth doing: a code-checking pass found seventeen factual errors (cluster rings are orange not cyan, the frame transform has no free scale, the three matchers all run every time rather than as a cascade, touching blobs are rejected as blended rather than split, the quad index is built at runtime, star names fall back to Bayer before HIP, a later *Detect Star Size* DOES overwrite a hand-edited `minArea`, the reported field of view is rectilinear-equivalent, roll about the optical axis is the unobservable rotation rather than a pan, short tracks are never drawn); a first-time-reader pass found where the writing misleads, including one passage it praised that turned out to be false — *Show rejected* displays classified tracks, not the detector's rejections, which are recorded but never drawn. Two sigmas are distinguished throughout, since the detect threshold measures brightness noise while everything judging movement measures positional scatter.
- **`docs/StarTracker-PriorWork.md` — prior work and an honest account of what is novel** (`df3ba5d5`, extended by `6b0d97fc`). Maps every stage of the pipeline to the established literature — source detection and photometry, frame registration, tracklet association, global refinement, rotation estimation, noise statistics, lens self-calibration, blind plate solving, clustering, and the planned sky segmentation — with a Match / Diverge / Extend / Local verdict for each and a closing shortlist of what could honestly be claimed as a contribution, with the caveats each would need. Unverifiable references are marked as such rather than dressed up. Corrects a factual error in `StarTracker.md` while it was there: the catalog is NOT the Bright Star Catalogue — it holds ~118,000 stars keyed by Hipparcos number down to about magnitude 12, where BSC5 has 9,110; the file is merely NAMED BSC5, and the shipped test asserts `catalog.n > 100000`, so the code always disagreed with the prose. `6b0d97fc` records what the sky-segmentation prototypes measured before the implementation was written, and is why `growSkyFromSeeds` is seeded rather than top-border-flooded: on a 4032x3024 twilight photograph local luma standard deviation is strongly bimodal (p75 0.82 against p90 13.55) and behaves as the literature predicts, but on 1280x720 image-intensifier video the dead vignette surround is the SMOOTHEST region in the frame (luma 0, texture 0.00 against the sky's 61.4 and 0.63) while filling 76% of the top row, and texture does not separate that clip at all (sky 0.63 against below-horizon 0.71) where brightness does (61.4 against 30.3). Conclusions carried into the shipped code: valid-region detection must precede sky reasoning and must not seed it, one texture cue cannot cover Sitrec's input range, and such a clip is a decline case where the honest output is a refusal rather than an invented horizon. Note the footnotes do not render in the built HTML — `webpack.common.js` constructs markdown-it with no plugins, so `[^ref]` markers appear literally; it degrades readably and is correct on GitHub, and fixing it needs `markdown-it-footnote`.
- **Satellite data publication delay, and refreshing a saved sitch** (`76043816`, merged as `720fc08e`; `docs/Starlink.md`, `docs/SavingAndLoading.md`, `docs/dev/FileRehosting.md`, `docs/dev/Installing-and-configuring.md`, `README.md`, `docs/CustomSitchTool.md`, `tools/shf/README.md`). Documents behaviour that shipped in 2.111.0. `Starlink.md` gains "Wait a few days before analysing a very recent event": orbital data is published a while after it applies, so loading a date only a day or two old gets roughly half the satellites on the same day, most by the next, all after three — which for a flare investigation is the difference between finding the responsible satellite and concluding there wasn't one, so it is stated as a workflow step rather than a footnote. The same section covers reopening a saved sitch: saving stores a copy of the satellite data, which is what makes an old sitch reproducible, but a sitch saved soon after an event stores one of those partial sets; Sitrec offers to fetch the rest, adding to what is loaded rather than replacing it, and changing nothing on the server until the user chooses to save. `SavingAndLoading.md` carries the same in the context of what a save contains, and `FileRehosting.md` notes that rehosting freezes a set in whatever state it was downloaded in. `Installing-and-configuring.md` gains a warning aimed at existing installs: the `CURRENT_STARLINK` / `CURRENT_ACTIVE` overrides take precedence over the built-in defaults, so an operator who set them previously is still requesting `FORMAT=tle`, and since the catalog passed 99999 on 2026-07-11 the TLE format cannot represent newer objects, which CelesTrak therefore omits — a catalog that looks fine but is quietly missing the newest satellites. Elsewhere, descriptions that said "TLE file" now say orbital data, in OMM CSV or legacy TLE, since both are read.

---

## Version 2.112.0 (2026-08-03)

### New Features
- **Images pasted from the clipboard import like dropped files** (`da925bd7`, `src/DragDropHandler.js`). Copying an image in a browser puts the decoded bytes straight on the clipboard, so pasting sidesteps the CORS wall that stops the same image being imported by dragging its URL in — the URL path has to `fetch()` the bytes, and most hosts send no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`. `onPaste` now calls a new `getPastedFiles(clipboardData)` **before** reading `text/plain`; the order is load-bearing, because Chrome puts a `text/html` `<img src>` on the clipboard alongside the bytes, so testing for text first took the URL branch and hit exactly the wall pasting exists to avoid. Files are read from `clipboardData.files`, falling back to walking `clipboardData.items` for `kind === 'file'`. Each one is handed to `uploadDroppedFile`, so pasting inherits the whole drop pipeline — content sniffing via `sniffFileType`, the *Video Image* / *Ground Overlay* choice, HEIC and JPEG 2000 decoding, EXIF, and non-image routing through `FileManager.parseAsset`. Pasting a KML works as a side effect. Browsers name a pasted image `image.<ext>`, which is uninformative in the file list and — since `FileManager.list` is keyed by filename — a collision on the second paste, so `renameGenericPastedFile` rewrites just those (regex `^image\.[a-z0-9]+$`; anything with a real name, such as a file copied in Finder, keeps it) to `pasted-image-<n>.<ext>`.
- **"Enough" — stop a long Star Tracker analysis and solve what has already been measured** (working tree; `src/CProgressIndicator.js`, `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`, `src/utils.js`). Video ▸ Star Tracker's detect pass walks every frame of the In/Out range before anything is solved, and on a long clip the only way out was *Abort*, which throws the whole run away. `initProgress` gains `showEnough` / `onEnough` / `enoughLabel`, mirroring the existing `showAbort` / `onAbort`: a green (`#4caf50`) button, `id="input-blocker-enough-button"`, inserted *before* the abort button rather than appended — the abort button outlives a run (it is only removed when a later caller asks for no abort), so a plain append would land below it on every run after the first. Its label is set on every call, not just on creation, so a later caller cannot be left showing the previous caller's wording; `hideProgress` clears `currentProgressEnoughCallback` alongside the abort one. In `StarTrackerUI.js` a module-level `enough` flag is checked at the top of the detect loop. It is deliberately **not** folded into `ctx.stale()` — `aborted` is (`stale: () => aborted || ...`), which makes the run `return null` and, in `runFullStarTracker`, skip `identifyStars` and `syncCameraToStarTrack` entirely; staleness means the answer is worthless. `enough` instead breaks the loop, keeps every frame measured so far, and runs the full solve on the shorter clip, so identify and camera sync still follow. The test is `if (enough && f > frame0)`: at the **top** of the iteration so it also short-circuits the `continue` taken when a frame yields no pixels, and never on the first frame because a solve needs at least one measured frame. `analysedLast = f - 1` is published as the result's `frame1` — the overlay and the pose lookup both map a global frame to an index via `frame0`, so they must describe the clip that was measured rather than the one requested — together with a `stoppedEarly` flag, and the *Status* line gains a `(stopped early, N/M frames)` suffix so a reader comparing the numbers against a full pass does not have to go to the console. Offered only for a genuine multi-frame pass (`showEnough: !still && total > 1`), which required moving the `still` test (`videoData instanceof CVideoImageData`) above `initProgress`. Label: *Enough (solve what we have)*. This also required a fix in `src/utils.js`: the input blocker's capture-phase `preventDefaultHandler` let clicks through for exactly one hardcoded id, `'input-blocker-abort-button'`, and swallowed clicks on anything else on that overlay, so the new button was inert until the allowlist became a set, `INPUT_BLOCKER_BUTTON_IDS`. Latent rather than shipped-broken — Abort was the only button the overlay had ever carried — but it is the trap any future overlay button falls into, hence the set and the comment.

### Bug Fixes
- **Fixed a dropped URL blaming the file when the real problem was that Sitrec could not read it** (`fb3d239d`, `src/DragDropHandler.js`). Dropping an image URL from a site that sends no CORS header showed *"URL did not resolve to a loadable video or file"* — wrong twice over: the file is there, and it is a perfectly good JPEG. The browser will happily *render* it in an `<img>`, but with no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` it will not let script read the bytes, and importing needs the bytes. The generic wording came from `uploadURL` collapsing every failure in one `try` block into a single dialog, so a network rejection read identically to a Facebook reel page with no video behind it — the case the message was actually written for. Failures that know better now attach `dropErrorTitle` / `dropErrorReason` to the thrown `Error`, and `showDroppedURLResolveError` prefers them, falling back to the old wording otherwise. A `fetch()` rejection (a `TypeError`, so there is no status to report) becomes *Cannot Read That URL*, names the missing CORS header as the likely cause, allows for the site being offline or unreachable, and points at the fix — save the file and drag that in. A non-ok response becomes *URL Not Available* and reports the actual `status` and `statusText`. The follow-on offer to stash the link in the notes is unchanged.
- **Fixed importing a file under an already-used name silently replacing the existing one** (working tree; `src/DragDropHandler.js`). `FileManager.list` is keyed by filename, so a second import under a name already present overwrote the first entry with no warning — sometimes the intent (re-importing a corrected file), sometimes silent data loss, so it now asks. `uploadDroppedFile` becomes a thin wrapper that awaits a new `resolveNameCollision(file)`, delegates to `importDroppedFile` (the previous body, otherwise unchanged), and releases the claimed name in a `finally`. On a collision `resolveNameCollision` raises a *Name Already Used* `showChoice` offering **Replace** (overwrite the imported file), **Keep Both** (import under `<base>-<n>.<ext>`, splitting on the LAST dot so `my.track.kml` keeps `my.track` as its base and a leading-dot name stays whole rather than becoming an empty base) and **Cancel** (returns `null`; nothing is imported). Putting the check at the one entry point every file import already shares means dragging, pasting, deferred sitch-browser drops and File ▸ *Import File* (`CFileManager.importFile`) all resolve a collision identically, and every downstream registration site sees the settled name; a dropped **URL** is not covered, since that path fetches the bytes and goes through `queueResult` instead. `FileManager.exists()` alone cannot settle a name, because an import does not register until well after one is chosen — it awaits the type sniffer, then a dialog answered at human speed — so two images in one paste, or a second import begun before the first dialog is answered, would both see `-1` as free and the later would overwrite the earlier. Names are therefore claimed in `reservedImportNames` (generalised from the paste-only `reservedPastedNames`) for the window between naming and registration, with `reserveUniqueName(base, ext)` now shared between collision renaming and pasted-image naming. Video and `parseResult` imports register after `uploadDroppedFile` returns, so their claim is dropped before they land, which leaves them behaving exactly as they did before name checking existed.

### Security
- **`brace-expansion` bumped past the unbounded-expansion DoS** (`c5e04dc9`, `package-lock.json`). GHSA-mh99-v99m-4gvg (high): `brace-expansion` can be driven to expand without bound and take the process out on memory. Two copies were in range — `node_modules/brace-expansion` 2.1.2 → 2.1.4 (`<2.1.3`) and `test-exclude/node_modules/brace-expansion` 1.1.16 → 1.1.18 (`<1.1.17`). Both are **dev-only**, reached through jest → test-exclude → glob → minimatch, so neither reaches the bundle or production; the exposure is a build or test machine handed a hostile glob pattern. Both are transitive, so this is lockfile-only — `package.json` is untouched. Recorded here because the discovery matters for next time: it was found by `npm audit`, **not** by Dependabot, whose five current alerts are all false — postcss, webpack-dev-server and body-parser are every one already patched in the committed lockfiles (8.5.23 against an alleged ≤8.5.17, 5.2.6 against ≤5.2.5, 2.3.0 against <2.3.0). Its dependency-graph snapshot is stale: the SBOM still reports postcss 8.5.10 and 1409 packages against 1011 actually installed, the same figure it gave five days and four releases earlier. The lockfile is what settles it.

## Version 2.111.1 (2026-08-02)

### Improvements
- **Star Track's spherical solve runs across a worker pool, and the whole analysis stops blocking the page** (`5ee17219`, `d77753a8`, `643310e5`; `src/starTrack/StarSolveSphere.js`, new `src/starTrack/StarSphereSolvePool.js`, new `src/workers/StarSphereWorker.js`, `src/starTrack/StarSolve.js`, `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`, new `tests/StarSphereSolveParallel.test.js`). A dense Milky Way timelapse (≈2450 tracks over 160 frames) took about six minutes to analyse and made Chrome offer to kill the page half a dozen times, while the progress bar sat at 96% reading *"Building star map"* for five and a half minutes — because the two stages actually running, `calibrateLens` and `refineGlobalSpherical`, had no progress reporting at all. Profiling first, since two earlier guesses at the cause were both wrong: `solveStarField` 4.5 s, `calibrateLens` 7.4 s, `refineGlobalSpherical#1` **206 s**, `#2` **95 s**. **(1) `obs.find()` inside a frames × tracks double loop**, in `refineGlobal` (`StarSolve.js`) and `refineGlobalSpherical` alike — O(frames × tracks × obs) EVERY iteration, and there are up to 40 of them in the 2D refinement and 12 in the spherical one, ≈8 million find-steps per iteration on this clip. The observations never change during refinement, so they are indexed by frame once (`byFrame`, and `packAnchors` on the sphere side). Order is preserved exactly — tracks ascending, first observation per frame, which is what `find()` returned — so the result is unchanged, only faster. **(2) An anchor cap on the SPHERICAL solve only.** A frame's orientation has three degrees of freedom; a few hundred well-observed stars pin it far below the measurement noise, and every track past that is paid for in every iteration of every frame. `STAR_SPHERE_DEFAULTS.maxAnchors: 400`, longest-observed first. It restricts only what BUILDS the orientations: every track still has its direction updated against them and `classifyTracksSpherical` still judges all of them, so nothing is dropped from the result, and on a sparse field where the cap does not bite nothing changes at all. The matching cap was added to the 2D solve at the same time and then **removed** — `STAR_SOLVE_DEFAULTS.maxAnchors` now defaults to `0` (off), a measured choice: with the index in place the three 2D refinements cost 1232 + 583 + 223 ms combined, so a cap saves nothing there, while it *cost* identification, because `identifyStars` reads the chart this map produces and a 400-anchor map is a worse chart — named stars on that clip fell 102 → 67, restored to 102 with the cap off. The knob remains for a caller that wants it. **(3) The spherical solve moved off the UI thread.** `refineGlobalSpherical` is split into phase kernels — `packObservations` / `packMap` / `packStates` / `packAnchors` for the flattened typed-array inputs, `fitFrameOrientation` per FRAME, `solveTrackDirection` / `updateMapChunk` / `finalRefsChunk` per TRACK, and `applyGauge` — driven either by the synchronous solve in the same file or by `refineGlobalSphericalAsync` (`StarSphereSolvePool.js`) across `min(8, hardwareConcurrency − 1)` workers, one core left for the UI. BOTH axes are parallel because the measured phase split demands it: per-frame orientation fit 65–76% (splits across frames), per-track direction update 12–19% and residual sum 10–16% (split across tracks), gauge re-pin <0.1%. Parallelising only the orientation fit — the obvious choice, since it is the single biggest term — caps the speedup at 1/(0.30 + 0.70/8) = **2.6× on eight cores**. `StarSphereWorker.js` is a `postMessage` shim that *imports the real kernels* via `new Worker(new URL("../workers/StarSphereWorker.js", import.meta.url))` — which webpack chunks per build mode with no config, as `ScriptRunnerWorker` and three others already do — rather than hand-inlining a second copy of Wahba, the pixel-domain rotation refinement and the lens mapping, which the inline-Blob pattern in `FastComputeVertexNormalsAsync.js` would have forced. The three-message protocol (`init` once per solve, `round` once per phase, `chunk` per range) keeps per-chunk traffic to a `[lo, hi)` pair, and every message carries the solve's `gen` so a late reply from a superseded run is dropped. **The result is bit-identical, verified end to end rather than assumed**: before and after builds produce the same FNV hash (`8286b6bc`) of all 160 per-frame quaternions and all 2531 track directions at full double precision, the same classification counts, and the 118-frame regression clip still gives its documented 231 star / 5 moving. The one number that moves is `rms`, by ~1e-15, because the cost is now reduced from per-track partials summed in track index order **in the synchronous path too** — floating-point addition is not associative and a flat running total is unreachable once tracks are spread across workers — which is 11 orders below the 1e-4 convergence gate and cannot reach a decision. **Progress and abort**: new stages `"Fitting camera lens"`, `"Solving sky rotation: pass 1, iteration N"` and `"Re-solving on stars only: iteration N"`, with `yieldToBrowser` between them, `calibrateLens` made `async` with an `onYield` hook, and `solveStarField` printing a per-stage timing breakdown so the next person does not have to guess either. The Abort button now takes effect during these stages — `refineGlobalSphericalAsync` is passed `shouldAbort: () => ctx.stale()`, and `ctx.stale()` includes the `aborted` flag — where previously they were one uninterruptible synchronous block in which the bar could not move and the button could not be clicked. **Measured**: `refineGlobalSpherical#1` 66.0 s → 4.8 s, `#2` 55.0 s → 5.3 s (from 206 s and 95 s before the index and cap), whole analysis about six minutes → 159 s → ~75–80 s. Both passes converge (8 and 9 iterations of a possible 12), so there is nothing to save by lowering `refineIterations`. Jest has no `Worker`, so the solve falls back to the synchronous path there and in any browser where a worker fails to spawn — the same kernels, so a fallback costs time and changes no result. `tests/StarSphereSolveParallel.test.js` pins lane-count invariance at 1, 2, 3, 5, 8 and 13 lanes, the exclude set, the abort path, and the `ref` write-back, which the async wrapper has to apply itself since a worker cannot mutate the caller's tracks and everything downstream reads `ref`.
- **Star circles are tiered by brightness, so a dense field stops burying the footage** (`d9e65ad7`, `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`). A rich Milky Way frame solves ~882 stars, and at full weight `drawStarTrackerOverlay` painted a wall of green over the video it was annotating. New `BRIGHT_CIRCLES = 100`: the hundred brightest draw as before (1.8 px stroke, full opacity), and every fainter star draws at 1 px and 0.25 alpha — still drawn, because *"why is that star not circled"* has to stay answerable by looking, just quietly enough to see through. The cutoff is a **magnitude threshold**, not a per-frame top-100: `brightMagnitudeCutoff()` sorts the star magnitudes once and returns the `BRIGHT_CIRCLES`-th brightest (`Infinity` when there are fewer), cached against the `result` object's identity rather than recomputed per frame — this is a render loop, and the sort plus its temporary array would otherwise run at frame rate for an answer that only changes when a new analysis replaces the old one. Because it is a fixed magnitude, a star's weight stays stable through frames in which it drops out of detection. An unmeasured magnitude counts as faint: a star with no photometry cannot claim to be in the top hundred. Only `klass === "star"` is demoted — movers are the point of the analysis and there are few of them, and the rejected classes are off by default. The disabled and faint states **multiply rather than merge** (`disabled ? (faint ? 0.15 : 0.3) : (faint ? 0.25 : ...)`) so a faint star still visibly changes when it is toggled out of the working set.

### Bug Fixes
- **Fixed lens calibration refusing cropped footage outright, leaving the flat 2D model to judge motion** (`7992b3aa`, `src/starTrack/StarCalibrate.js`, `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`, new `tests/StarCalibrateCroppedClip.test.js`, new `tests/fixtures/croppedStarlinkClip.json`, `docs/dev/star-identify-edge-stars.md`). A cropped Starlink timelapse came back **283 star / 658 moving** — most of the real stars called movers — because `calibrateLens` declined with *"no better than a rectilinear lens (rms 11.70 vs 11.97)"*. Both numbers are terrible: nothing fitted at all, so the pinhole assumption stood and the 2D chain's frame-edge bias was read as motion. The data was never the problem. The clip is a CROP of a larger frame, so its optical axis is at **(953, 239)** of the 1280×720 video, not the frame centre (640, 360) — measured independently from a source image and never shown to the fit. Scoring lenses directly, the frame centre gives rms 6.82 px with 41/153 inliers, while the true axis gives a plain rectilinear f = 1150 at **rms 0.53 with 153/153**: one rotation through a pinhole explains every correspondence to half a pixel. Three independent causes, each of which alone produced the same symptom. **(1) No local search can find that axis.** `refinePrincipal` is a descent, and the rms surface over the principal point — with lens type and focal re-fitted at every cell, so this is not an artifact of holding them fixed — has its global minimum 0.60 at the true axis, while from the frame centre (5.90) the steepest improvement points *away* from it into a broad shallow plateau. New `scanPrincipal` takes a coarse global look first: an n × n grid (`principalScanSteps: 5`, odd so the centre is included) across ±`principalMaxOffsetFrac`, scored on a subsample (`principalScanPairs: 40`) at coarse focal resolution (`principalScanFocals: 9`) over two bracketing shapes (`principalScanTypes: ["rectilinear", "orthographicFisheye"]`, a pinhole and the most compressive fisheye — across the grid the principal point dominates the score and the exact curve barely moves it). It is **gated** on `diag.centredWithin`, the fraction of the input correspondences the centred scan explains, falling below `principalSearchWithinFrac: 0.6`, so a centred clip never pays for the grid: the uncropped reference clip scores 0.99 and skips it, this one scores 0.35. It **nominates** its top `principalScanKeep: 3` cells rather than committing to one, because at that focal resolution its own ranking is unreliable — re-scanning only its winner accepted a principal point 77 px from truth, seeded on the grid's own boundary, where at the full 34 focals the near-truth cell wins outright (144/153 at rms 1.186 against 97/153 at rms 3.121). Each nominee is re-scanned at full resolution and adopted only on strictly better evidence, more inliers AND rms no worse than 1.05×, since a noisy *centred* clip can still trip the trigger; `diagnostics.principalScanAtBoundary` reports a winner sitting on the grid edge. **(2) `chooseBaseline` took 31 correspondences over 342.** It returned the FIRST baseline clearing `minPairs`, walking widest-inwards, on the theory that the widest baseline has the most signal. Width alone is not signal: on this clip that rule took a 127-frame baseline with 31 pairs over a 40-frame one with 342, and 31 noisy pairs gave rms 2.05 with a principal point 268 px out, where the richer baseline gives rms 0.51 and lands within 7 px. Candidates are now ranked by `pairs * span` — but screened for **rotation first**, with a cheap nominal-lens `fitRotationRobust` at 0.8 × `minRotationDeg`, because rotation is a prerequisite rather than a tiebreak and the gate enforcing it only ever sees the ONE chosen baseline, so a dense but static baseline could refuse a clip whose wider one calibrates (measured on a 1° synthetic scene: 0→12 with 150 pairs wins on score, rotates 0.31° and is refused, while 0→39 with 25 pairs rotates 0.99° and calibrates). The widest-available baseline is kept as a fallback so a clip that never reaches `minPairs` still reports its best effort and the caller's own "only N correspondences" refusal is what speaks. **(3) The holdout gate refused correct answers by construction.** It compared the chosen lens's **held-out** rms against the rectilinear fit's **in-sample** rms. Out-of-sample error is essentially always larger, so whenever the true lens really is a pinhole — which a cropped phone clip is — it refused: measured on this clip, held-out 0.66 against an in-sample rectilinear 0.50, refused, no lens, frame-edge stars left red. Both sides are now measured on the same held-out half (a shared `heldOut(lens)` closure, reported as `diag.holdoutRectRms`), and a free polynomial that still fails the gate **falls back to the preset fit underneath it** (`presetRefined`, recorded in `diag.customRejected`) rather than taking the whole calibration down — a polynomial that does not generalise is a verdict on the polynomial, not on the clip. **Recovered against truth the fit never sees**: (955, 246) and (949, 246) on the two well-populated baselines, 7–8 px on an axis 336 px off centre; in the app a fitted (938, 242) at 16 px, 57.2° hFOV, and **889 star / 44 moving** where it was 283 / 658. This supersedes the "severe crops fail safe, the gate declines and the pinhole stands" note in 2.111.0's **Camera ▸ Lens** entry as far as real cropped footage goes: the crop that used to be refused now calibrates to within a few pixels. Also in this commit: `calibrateLens` is `async` and takes an `onYield` hook (`StarTrackerUI` passes `yieldToBrowser`) so the page can breathe between stages; `diag.focalPx` refreshes when the free-shape search moves it; the custom-to-preset fallback no longer reports the discarded fit's `principalClamped`, which would have told the user a clean calibration was CLAMPED; and `centredWithin` counts input correspondences rather than the rows a lens happened to be able to project, since `scoreLens` skips pairs whose ray or reprojection is undefined and reports `n` for the rest, so `within/n` could read 0.75 for a lens that explained 15 of 100 pairs and evaluated 20. New `tests/StarCalibrateCroppedClip.test.js` is the first calibration test with an independently measured answer rather than a synthetic truth or a self-consistency check, including one asserting the data was always fine. Reviewed by Codex; known and documented, not fixed: gate 5 is still not an independent holdout (every outer-test row influenced both geometries), held-out rms can be computed over different subsets per model, `pairs * span` is not an information objective (`N * span²` would be), a noisy centred clip can still trigger the search, and the yields do not make the page properly responsive because `refineCustom` is a single ~14 s descent with none inside it.
- **Fixed the overlay circles still being placed by the flat 2D model the lens fit exists to replace** (`d9e65ad7`, `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`, `docs/dev/star-track-overlay-circles.md`). `drawStarTrackerOverlay` placed every circle with `applyTransform(T, rx, ry)` — the per-frame 2D similarity from the 2D chain. That is four degrees of freedom (one rotation, one uniform scale, one shift), so it moves the whole field of circles **rigidly** and cannot bend, while a wide lens does bend: θ(ρ) is nonlinear (this clip fits θ = ρ(1 − 0.31ρ² + 0.10ρ⁴)), so one sky rotation moves an edge star a different number of *pixels* than a centre star, and a single scale for the whole image can only be right at one radius. The error therefore grows outward **from the optical axis**, which is why the user-reported symptom was asymmetric: on a cropped clip whose fitted axis sits at x ≈ 941 of 1280, stars on the LEFT drifted out of their green circles while those on the RIGHT stayed centred. Measured at frame 84 over 231 observed stars, median pixel error against the actual detections, by distance from the optical axis — 0–200 px: **0.30** (2D) vs **0.17** (sphere); 200–400: 0.39 vs 0.19; 400–600: **3.10** vs 0.23; 600–800: **7.42** vs 0.17; 800–1000: **11.35** vs 0.33 — worst case 23.7 px against 0.89 px, on circles of radius 6–24 px, so out at the edge the old placement missed by a full circle width. Adding degrees of freedom does not rescue a planar model: a homography (8 dof) measured 11.4 px against the similarity's 11.7, because `K R K⁻¹` models perspective and radial compression is not a projective map. **The fix** places each star from its own settled direction through that frame's solved orientation — `refToFrame(states[i], lens, track.ref, [result.videoW, result.videoH])`, reading `states` off `result.lensInfo` — whenever the lens was fitted, keeping the 2D chart as the fallback for runs where it was not. Movers hop **on the sphere** instead, `framePixelToFrame(states[o.f], states[i], lens, o.x, o.y, size)` from the nearest observation, because a mover's `ref` is one settled direction and using it would pin the marker still, which is exactly the motion the red circle exists to show. A null projection — the lens says the ray has no image in this frame — now **skips** the circle (`noImage`), where the 2D model always returned a point, which is how a circle ends up drawn where the star cannot be; a merely missing direction still falls back to the 2D chart. After the fix all 882 stars on that clip are placed spherically, none skipped, median error 0.21 px and worst 1.0 px. Two 2D holdouts remain **deliberately**, and are recorded in `docs/dev/star-track-overlay-circles.md`: star **identification** (`identifyStars`) still consumes the 2D reference chart, because it is calibrated against the star set that chart produced and handing it the recovered edge stars broke its match consensus; and light **clusters** stay on the 2D placement, because a cluster's motion model lives in reference-chart coordinates and there is no chart-to-direction map to carry it onto the sphere — the chart is a chain of similarities, not a projection of one — and the inherited edge error is small against a ring whose radius is the formation's own extent (≥ 20 px). The same doc corrects the earlier "attempt made and reverted, reason unknown" note: the approach was never wrong (that exact projection lands sub-pixel on every star), so the revert was an implementation slip, most likely the wrong `size` — it has to be the ANALYSED decode size `result.videoW/H`, or `lensScaleFor` rescales everything about the principal point — or passing `lensInfo.chart[i]`, a 2D gnomonic position, where a 3-vector direction was wanted.

## Version 2.111.0 (2026-08-02)

### New Features
- **Star Track fits the camera lens and judges motion on the celestial sphere** (`ad6e4523`, `854f871a`; new `src/CameraLens.js`, `src/starTrack/StarSphere.js`, `StarSolveSphere.js`, `StarCalibrate.js`, `StarSyntheticSphere.js`; `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`). Star Track modelled frame-to-frame sky motion as a 2D similarity in pixel space — the small-angle, narrow-field approximation to what actually happens, which is a rotation of the celestial sphere seen through a lens. On a wide field that approximation is biased at the frame edges and the bias is reported as motion. Measured on the reference IR-monocular timelapse (celestial pole just past the top-right corner): the 2D model explains 84 of 129 star correspondences with an 11.7 px worst case, under-reports the sky rotation as 2.19° against a true 3.28°, and calls 70 real stars moving; rays through a fitted lens plus ONE 3D rotation explain all 129 under 2.5 px. A homography barely helps (11.4 px) — `K R K⁻¹` covers perspective, but radial lens compression is not a projective map, so no planar model of any order absorbs it. New **Fit lens from stars** toggle (default ON) and a **Lens** status line at the top of Video → Star Tracker, backed by `calibrateLens` (`StarCalibrate.js`), `statesFromChain2D` / `attachRays` / `refineGlobalSpherical` / `classifyTracksSpherical` (`StarSolveSphere.js`), and a quaternion per-frame state with Wahba + pixel refinement (`StarSphere.js`). The 2D solve is kept as the bootstrap and proposal layer — it decides which detections belong together and supplies the chart the overlay draws in, and its ~10 px edge error is nothing beside a 6–24 px circle — so only the VERDICT is recomputed on the sphere (`c.klass2D` preserves the 2D call). Camera-fixed artifacts stay the 2D pass's decision: `classifyTracksSpherical` has no artifact test and a hot pixel holds its PIXEL position while the sky rotates, so re-judging it there turns dust and a reticle into confident movers; artifacts, and then the movers, are also excluded from the fit used to judge them, the same circularity the 2D solver avoids with its star-only re-solve. Calibration **fails closed**: a pure roll about the optical axis constrains no lens whatsoever (every radial lens maps it to the same rotation about the principal point), and the identifiability gate rejects it however large and clean it looks, along with still cameras, thin baselines, fields with no radial motion, and anything a re-optimised rectilinear model already explains — so leaving the toggle on costs nothing on narrow-field footage. `854f871a` then refines the winning preset into a free **custom polynomial** (d3, d5, d7) seeded by a *least-squares* fit to the preset's own curve over the radii the image actually uses (a truncated Taylor series is worse where it matters: 0.98 px corner error vs 9.83 px for an orthographic lens over 1280×720), with each added term held out so it must earn its degree of freedom on correspondences it never saw. New tests measure ABSOLUTE sky accuracy against known truth, not just self-consistency — including one that pins the trap that a lens 12% off in focal length still fits its own data respectably while getting the sky measurably wrong. The synchronous shape search is bounded (`shapeMaxPairs` 110, `shapeScales`, `shapeMaxSteps`) after it froze the browser for minutes on the reference clip; note that `shapeMaxPairs` trades speed against SHAPE accuracy specifically — halving the pairs took absolute sky error from 0.09° to 0.30° while the self-consistency rms barely moved. Live on the reference clip: **231 stars / 5 movers, where it previously gave 169 / 70**. Star identification deliberately stays on the 2D chart and the star set that chart produced (see the Identify fix below). 74 new tests in `tests/CameraLens.test.js`, `StarSphere.test.js`, `StarSolveSphere.test.js`, `StarCalibrate.test.js`, `StarSolveDriftStatistic.test.js`, `StarSphereRealClip.test.js`, with the real clip's measured correspondences as a fixture.
- **Camera ▸ Lens — the fitted lens, reported where a lens belongs** (`78965daa`, `src/index.js`, `src/starTrack/StarTrackerUI.js`, `src/starTrack/StarCalibrate.js`, `tests/StarCalibrate.test.js`). Field of view does not describe a camera: it says how much sky the frame covers, not how that sky is laid out across the pixels — two cameras with the same 96° horizontal field put a star 40° off axis in completely different places if one is a pinhole and the other a fisheye, and that difference is the whole reason the flat model called 70 real stars "moving". Star Track's calibration is the only thing in Sitrec that ever lifts the pinhole assumption, so a new permanent **Lens** folder is added beside **FOV (Zoom)** (`addGUIFolder("cameraLens", "Lens", "camera")`, joined to the `updateCameraFolders` show-if-populated list) and filled by `updateCameraLensMenu()`: *Type*, *Focal length*, *Principal point*, *Axis off centre*, *Distortion (d3, d5, d7)*, *Horizontal / Vertical / Diagonal FOV*, *Fit residual* (with the held-out figure, which is the one that says whether it generalises), and a *Copy lens JSON* action. Hidden until a lens is fitted, and cleared by `resetStarTracker()`, so a sitch with no calibration grows no dead folder and the previous clip's optics never linger describing footage that is gone. Read-only deliberately — these are measurements, and a hand-edited focal length would silently disagree with the classification, the chart and the camera sync computed from the fitted one. **Cropping**: `refinePrincipal` clamped the principal point to ±25% of frame with a comment calling an off-centre axis "a fitting artifact, not an optical property", which is exactly backwards for cropped footage, and the same constant was duplicated in `refineCustom` so the two searches could disagree. A centred digital zoom is harmless (the crop keeps the axis at the new centre, and `focalPx` is measured in the analysed pixels either way), but an UNEVEN crop — a 16:9 window off one side of the sensor, a re-framed export, a stabilised sub-rectangle — moves the optical axis by however much was taken off that side, and a hard enough crop puts it outside the frame altogether. The bound stays (the principal point is weakly observable and an unbounded search wanders instead of converging) but is now one shared option, `principalMaxOffsetFrac`, widened to 0.45 and REPORTED via `diagnostics.principalClamped` when the fit ends up pressed against it: the menu reads "— OUTSIDE THE FRAME" when the axis lands off-image and "— CLAMPED, may be cropped further" when the search wanted to keep going, so a clamped value reads as a floor rather than as a measurement. Measured on synthetic cropped 1280×720 clips: a centred axis and a −140,−60 offset both recover to 5 px, −280,−110 to 65 px, and −380,−160 and −540,−240 are REFUSED with no lens fitted — severe crops fail safe, the gate declines and the pinhole stands. Also fixes a reporting bug this exposed: the free-shape refinement moves the principal point AFTER `diag.principal` was recorded, so the reported optical axis was the preset stage's — 170 px from the one actually returned on the cropped test scene, and it is the returned one the user is shown; `diag.principal` / `principalOffset` / `principalClamped` are now re-read after the custom stage.
- **Satellites read the CCSDS OMM keyword set in CSV, not TLE** (`0fd2bfb0`, `e5459254`; `src/TLEUtils.ts`, `src/CFileManagerParse.js`, `src/SatelliteSearch.js`, `src/nodes/CSatellite.js`, new `sitrecServer/gpData.php`, `sitrecServer/proxy.php`, `sitrecServer/proxyStarlink.php`, `config/shared.env.example`, `tools/shf/`). The TLE format cannot express catalog numbers above 99999, and the catalog passed that limit on 2026-07-11. CelesTrak and Space-Track do not work around it with Alpha-5 — they omit those objects from TLE feeds entirely — so Sitrec was already losing satellites: measured against the CelesTrak Starlink supplemental feed on 2026-08-01, TLE carried 10,770 objects and CSV 10,898, i.e. **128 missing, including 24 with 9-digit IDs, and all of them recent launches**. Every element source now requests `FORMAT=csv`. OMM CSV has no catalog-number limit, keeps the full-precision epoch that TLE rounds, and is marginally SMALLER on the wire than TLE (1.762 MB vs 1.809 MB; OMM JSON is 2.8× and XML 6×, so CSV is the right serialization); parsing costs 2.6 µs/sat via `json2satrec` against 2.2 µs for `twoline2satrec`, ~5 ms across 10.9k satellites, and CSV and TLE agree on propagated position to 3.4 m mean / 7.1 m max — that gap being TLE's epoch rounding, so CSV is the more accurate of the two. TLE parsing is kept throughout: old cached files, saved sitches and user-supplied `.tle` files all still load. Parsing details: `CTLEData` now reads the file as a sequence of same-format BLOCKS rather than sniffing only the first line, because `mergeFrom` concatenates the raw text it exports and a user can merge an imported `.tle` into a downloaded CSV catalogue, so an export can legitimately hold both formats — sniffing once fed one block to the wrong parser and silently dropped those satellites on reload (3 in, 2 out); two CSV sets merge into one valid CSV by dropping the second header; blank lines at block edges are trimmed since the 3LE-vs-2LE test reads `lines[1]`/`lines[2]`; `noradIndex` becomes a `Map` (assigning index 799,501,305 into an array is memory-safe but gives `.length` a value of ~8e8); TLE names are trimmed of their fixed-width padding so they match CSV names feeding the same lookups; and `parseOMMCSVLines` is shared with `SatelliteSearch` rather than duplicated. Dropping an OMM CSV onto Sitrec routes to the satellite path rather than the track parsers (`isOMMCSV` on the header line only, not a split of the whole file). Exports are named for what they hold — `satelliteExportFilename()` writes `satellites.csv` for an OMM set and `satellites.tle` otherwise, with a MIXED set named `.tle` deliberately, because that route always reaches `CTLEData` whereas the `.csv` route requires line 1 to be an OMM header. Server-side, the new `gpData.php` (`isValidGPData` / `writeGPCache` / `serveGPCached`) is shared by both proxies; payloads are gzipped once at fetch time and served with `Content-Encoding` plus ETag/304 (1.72 MB → 497 KB, 3.54×, decompressed by the browser), so new downloads need no JSZip and `TLE_ZIP_ENABLED` now governs only the legacy `.tle` files already in the cache; the CelesTrak cache lifetime becomes 2 hours to match its publication cadence; and `proxyStarlink.php` keeps serving a cached `.tle` for dates before the 2026-07-11 cutover, so the existing archive is not invalidated. `e5459254` then made the historical (Space-Track `gp_history`) path work with CSV at all: Space-Track's CSV is 40 columns with EVERY data field quoted (including a free-text `COMMENT` and `TLE_LINE0/1/2`), against CelesTrak's 19 unquoted, so a naive split on `,` read a catalog number as the 7-character string `"44714"` — quote marks included — which `Number()` reads as `NaN`; measured against a real `gp_history` response for 2026-07-20, **43,662 rows in, 0 satellites out**. `splitCSVRow()` now follows RFC 4180 (quoted fields, commas inside quotes, `""` as an escaped quote, tolerated trailing `\r`); columns were already resolved by name, so the differing count and ordering need no further handling, and the same 43,662 rows now yield 43,662 satellites. The same commit makes the historical cache **cache-first unconditionally** — Space-Track rate-limits hard, the archive goes back to 1977, and Sitrec reads either format, so any cached copy is used whatever format it is in and Space-Track is contacted only when we hold nothing (a second request for 2026-07-20 returns byte-identical data in 0.10 s rather than 4.0 s; 2013-08-11 LEO still serves its legacy `.tle.zip` in 0.01 s). `config/shared.env.example` now sets `FORMAT=csv`; note that `config/shared.env` overrides the proxy defaults and `injectEnv.php` reads the build-generated `shared.env.php`, so a config edit needs a rebuild — a deployment still pinning `FORMAT=tle` silently gets the old, incomplete catalogue. `tools/shf/flareEngine.js` gained the same block-wise reader and a CSV CelesTrak fallback URL: SHF hits the Sitrec proxy first, so without it SHF would fall through to direct CelesTrak and lose both the proxy and the newest Starlinks — the worst possible subset for a flare tool. Tests: 18 in `tests/CTLEData.omm.test.js`, 24 in `tools/shf/tools/test-omm.mjs`, covering 6- and 9-digit catalog numbers, CSV/TLE position agreement, malformed and concatenated input, merge/export/reload in both orders, a verbatim quoted `gp_history` row, an `OBJECT_NAME` containing a comma, and the embedded `TLE_LINE` columns not leaking in as extra satellites; the mixed-format cases were confirmed to fail against the sniff-once parser.
- **Offer to refresh the satellite data baked into your own saved sitches** (`b4831b6d`, `82a2c66d`; new `src/TLERefresh.js`, `src/TLEUtils.ts`, `src/CustomManagerSerialize.js`, `src/CFileManagerParse.js`, `src/nodes/CSatellite.js`, `tests/GPSetProvenance.test.js`). Saving a sitch rehosts the loaded element set as a static file, so reloading replays exactly what it was saved with — that is what makes an old sitch reproducible and it stays that way; nothing here deletes or overwrites the original. But Space-Track publishes elements AFTER their epoch and these queries span [D, D+2], so a set downloaded soon after the event it covers is truncated: reconstructed for a real date, a set holds 8.9% of the window at D+0.2 days, 40.6% at D+1.0, 96.8% at D+2.0, and is complete only from about D+2.5 — a sitch saved a day after the event bakes in a partial catalogue potentially missing hundreds of satellites, conceivably including the one the analysis is about. `checkAndOfferTLERefresh()` runs from `CustomManagerSerialize` AFTER the sitch is fully loaded and interactive, un-awaited so an open dialog never holds up the load and guarded on `Globals.loadGeneration` so a sitch the user has navigated away from does not prompt. Only the sitch's OWNER is asked (`isOwnLoadedSitch`, first path segment of the sitch reference vs `getEffectiveUserID()`) because it changes what the sitch loads and re-saving is the natural follow-up. Truncation is detected by `isGPSetIncomplete` from epoch COVERAGE — the newest epoch in the set falling short of the [D, D+2] window end — and deliberately NOT from `CREATION_DATE`, which is only a lower bound on when we downloaded, so an early value means either that we caught the set mid-publication OR that publication simply finished early; the two are indistinguishable and the second is common (it produced a false positive on the first real file tested). Coverage measures the truncation itself, survives the copying and rehosting that destroy file mtimes, and works on TLE-format bakes, which carry no `CREATION_DATE` at all. Reissuing the request needs the query, which a baked file does not record, so `CTLEData.inferQueryType()` reads it back off the signature each filter leaves in the data — STARLINK (every name starts `STARLINK`), SLOW (nothing in the fast mean-motion band), LEO (all fast, all `PAYLOAD`) and LEOALL (all fast, mixed object types); LEO and LEOALL are separable only when `OBJECT_TYPE` is present (i.e. OMM CSV), and for a legacy TLE bake the narrower LEO is taken, because refreshing MERGES so a narrower query can only under-repair whereas a broader one would add debris the user never had. Confirmed against three real cached sets, all correctly identified. Going forward the query is recorded outright: `CSatellite` tags a dated fetch with `{date, type}` and the save path serialises it as `tleSource`. The refresh fetches into a DISTINCT id (`starLink_<date>-refreshed.<ext>`) so the original stays in `FileManager` and the saved sitch keeps working until the user chooses to re-save, and `parseResult` gained an `options.trackOptions` passthrough so it can force `tleAction: "merge"` instead of re-asking a question the user just answered. Merge rather than replace, by catalog number: the user may have deliberately combined several sets. After a successful refresh a second dialog offers *Save sitch now* or *I'll check it first*. `82a2c66d` then stopped naming the refreshed file `.tle` while it held OMM CSV — harmless to the parser (`CTLEData` sniffs, and the `.tle` route reaches it regardless) but the rehosted name persists in the saved sitch, so anyone downloading it later got a `.tle` full of CSV (confirmed on a real save). `refreshedExtension()` now sniffs the response: `"PK"` → `tle` (a legacy zipped snapshot from an old cache entry), an OMM header → `csv`, otherwise `tle`.
